They apply repeated local unitary operations to an auxiliary modulator qubit, exploiting a Zeno-like mechanism to dynamically reshape the effective Hamiltonian and switch the charger-battery coupling on and off. They demonstrate this in a minimal three-body model and extend to a collective many-body architecture.

### Result

The protocol works beyond the ideal fast-control limit, preserves the collective charging power enhancement scaling with the number of battery units, and is feasible in an NV-13C spin platform.

### Conclusion

Modulator-assisted Zeno control offers a scalable route to regulating energy transfer in quantum batteries, addressing a key challenge in quantum thermodynamics.

*Keywords: quantum battery, Zeno effect, energy transfer control, quantum thermodynamics, NV center*

- **Link**: [https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-07-29-2176/](https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-07-29-2176/)
- **Tags**: `qubit`  `noise`  `dynamical codes`  `quantum error correction`  `measurement noise`

### TL;DR

This paper optimizes measurement schedules for dynamical color codes under biased noise, introducing the teraquop volume metric and showing that repeating measurements helps in measurement-biased regimes.

### Motivation

Dynamical stabilizer codes may enable large-scale quantum computation, but their performance depends on the measurement schedule, especially when measurements are noisy. The authors aim to optimize schedules for hardware with noisy readouts.

### Method

They analyze dynamically condensed color codes under various noise biases, using the teraquop volume (product of qubits and measurement rounds) to compare performance. They also compare decoding strategies: minimum-weight perfect matching vs. belief matching with correlated errors.

### Result

Repeating measurements improves performance under measurement-biased noise, but offers little benefit for unbiased or X-biased noise. The teraquop volume reveals that differences are mainly due to measurement rounds, not qubit count. Belief matching can turn a worst-performing code into a best-performing one.

### Conclusion

The teraquop volume is a valuable metric for assessing code performance, and optimizing measurement schedules and decoders is crucial for practical quantum error correction with noisy measurements.

*Keywords: dynamical codes, quantum error correction, measurement noise, teraquop volume, belief matching*

### Motivation

Accurate estimation of logical error rates is crucial for assessing quantum error correction performance, but existing methods can be sample-inefficient, especially at low noise.

### Method

The authors define a ratio of partition functions corresponding to maximum likelihood decoding and compare it with an order probability for a probabilistic decoder. They analyze the toric code under bitflip noise via the Random Bond Ising Model and also consider the color code.

### Result

They show that the partition function ratio differs from the order probability, and that estimating via these probabilities is more sample-efficient than counting decoder failures. They also discuss degeneracy effects and ensembling.

### Conclusion

This paper characterizes the quantum computational complexity of simulating classical linear dynamics with geometrically local interactions, showing no exponential quantum advantage for short-time dynamics but potential super-polynomial advantage for long-time dynamics under space constraints.

### Motivation

The authors aim to determine whether quantum computers can provide exponential speedups for simulating classical systems with local interactions, which are common in real-world PDEs, as opposed to the previously studied long-range interactions.

### Method

They dequantize the quantum algorithm for short-time dynamics, prove a lower bound relating it to probabilistic classical computation, and analyze the complexity of long-time dynamics in terms of quantum complexity classes.

### Result

Short-time dynamics simulation has no exponential quantum advantage and is as hard as polynomial-time linear-space probabilistic computation; long-time dynamics is captured by exponential-time polynomial-space quantum computation, suggesting super-polynomial time advantage under space restrictions.

### Conclusion

## 387. The Paradox Of Steady-state Light Diffusion

- **Authors**: André Liemert, Lorenzo Pattelli, Alwin Kienle, Federico Tommasi And Fabrizio Martelli
- **Link**: [https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1361-6633/ae8eaa](https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1361-6633/ae8eaa)
- **Tags**: `transport`  `scattering`  `light diffusion`  `radiative transfer equation`  `diffusion coefficient`

### TL;DR

This paper resolves the long-standing debate on the diffusion coefficient in light transport, proving that in steady-state with absorption no scalar diffusion coefficient can asymptotically match the radiative transfer equation.

### Motivation

The definition of the diffusion coefficient in light transport has been debated for decades, with absorption-dependent formulations violating scaling properties of the RTE. The authors aim to resolve this controversy through an exact consistency test.

### Method

The authors use analytical proofs and exact consistency tests based on the requirement that diffusion converges to the RTE in the limit of infinite propagation, analyzing both time-domain and continuous-wave regimes.

### Result

They prove that in the time domain, an absorption-independent coefficient is necessary and sufficient for exact long-time convergence, while in the continuous-wave regime with nonzero absorption, no scalar diffusion coefficient can make diffusion asymptotically equivalent to the RTE.

### Conclusion

The time-domain formulation is established as the only self-consistent reference, calling for re-examination of diffusion applications in optical transport studies.

*Keywords: light diffusion, radiative transfer equation, diffusion coefficient, absorption, steady-state*

### Conclusion

The cosmic dipole anomaly may indicate a need to revise the standard cosmological model, but further investigation is required to rule out systematic errors.
